G.2
Correctness of
synthesise

The
\mathsf{toAsrt}
function is correct in the following sense:

Theorem G.3

(Correctness of
\mathsf{toAsrt}
)
.

\mathsf{toAsrt}(\hat{\sigma})=P\implies\forall\varepsilon,s,h.~(\varepsilon(\hat{\sigma})=\sigma\Rightarrow\varepsilon(\hat{\theta}^{\text{id}}_{\varepsilon}),\sigma\models P)\land(\varepsilon(\hat{\theta}^{\text{id}}_{\varepsilon}),(s,h)\models P\Rightarrow\varepsilon(\hat{\sigma})=(s|_{\hat{\sigma}.\mathsf{sto}},h))

where: the
identity substitution

\hat{\theta}^{\text{id}}_{\varepsilon}
, turning logical variables into symbolic variables, is injective and defined as
\hat{\theta}^{\text{id}}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\hat{x}
, with
\mathop{\mathrm{codom}}(\hat{\theta}^{\text{id}}_{\varepsilon})=\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}(\varepsilon)
;
\hat{\sigma}.\mathsf{sto}
denotes the store of
\hat{\sigma}
; and
s|_{\hat{s}}
denotes the store
s
with domain restricted to the domain of
\hat{s}
.

Proof of Thm.
9
.

\textsf{synthesise}(f,\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P)
with
f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})\{\mathsf{C};\mathtt{return}\ E\}
is guaranteed to end successfuly with a set of triples
\left\{(o_{i},\hat{\sigma}_{i},\hat{h}_{i})\right\}
, which in the worst case scenario will simply be empty. The following assumptions form the hypothesis of our theorem:

(S1)

\hat{\sigma}=(\hat{s},\hat{h},\hat{\pi})

(S1a)

\hat{\theta}(x)=\hat{x}
for all
x\in\mathtt{lv}(\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P)
, else undefined

(S1b)

\hat{s}(\vec{\mathsf{x}})=\vec{\hat{x}}
and
\hat{s}(\vec{\mathsf{z}})=\mathtt{null}
where
\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{pv}(\mathsf{C})\setminus\{\vec{\mathsf{x}}\}
, else undefined

(S1c)

\hat{h}=\emptyset

(S1d)

\hat{\pi}={\sf true}

(S2)

\textsf{produce}(P,\hat{\theta},\hat{\sigma})\rightsquigarrow\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}

(S3)

\hat{\sigma}^{\prime},\mathsf{C}\Downarrow_{\Gamma}^{\text{bi}}\hat{\Sigma}^{\prime}

(S4)

\textsf{synthesise}(\Gamma,f,P)=\left\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{\mathit{x}}\mathrel{\star}P_{i}\end{array}\right]}\ {f(\vec{\mathsf{x}})}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[o:\ \begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\exists\vec{\mathit{y}}.~Q_{i}\end{array}\right]}\right\}
such that:

(S4a)

(o_{i},(\hat{s}_{i}^{\prime},\hat{h}_{i}^{\prime},\hat{\pi}_{i}^{\prime}),\hat{h}_{i}^{\prime}))\in\Sigma^{\prime}

(S4b)

P_{i}=P\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{toAsrt}((\emptyset,\hat{h}_{i},{\sf true}))

(S4c)

Q_{i}=\mathsf{toAsrt}(\hat{s}_{i}^{\prime}|_{\{\mathsf{x}\}},\hat{h}_{i}^{\prime},\hat{\pi}_{i}^{\prime})
with
\mathsf{x}=\mathsf{ret}
if
o_{i}=\mathit{ok}
,
\mathsf{x}=\mathsf{err}
otherwise.

(S4d)

\vec{\mathit{y}}=\mathtt{lv}(Q_{i})\setminus\mathtt{lv}(P_{i})

We start by showing that
\exists\vec{\mathit{y}}.~Q_{i}
is a UX internalisation of
Q_{i}^{\prime}=\mathsf{toAsrt}(\hat{s}_{i}^{\prime},\hat{h}_{i}^{\prime},\hat{\pi}_{i}^{\prime})
, i.e, that they satisfy the following internalisations conditions:

(IC1)

\mathsf{E}\in\mathsf{Val}\Leftarrow Q_{i}^{\prime}\star\mathsf{True}
if
o_{i}=\mathit{ok}

(IC2)

Q_{i}\Leftarrow\exists\vec{\mathit{p}}.Q_{i}^{\prime}[\vec{\mathit{p}}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]\star\mathsf{ret}=\mathsf{E}{}[\vec{\mathit{p}}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]
if
o_{i}=\mathit{ok}

(IC3)

Q_{\mathit{err}}\Leftarrow\exists\vec{\mathit{p}}.Q_{\mathit{err}}^{\prime}[\vec{\mathit{p}}/\vec{\mathsf{p}}]
if
o_{i}=\mathit{err}

Internalisation conditions 1-3.

These properties, in the case
o_{i}=\mathit{ok}
, naturally follow from Thm.
G.3
as well as (S4), and the fact that
\mathsf{ret}:=\mathsf{E}
is executed in each branch before the call to
toAsrt
.

We want to show that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}P\star\vec{\mathsf{x}}=\vec{\mathit{x}}\star\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}\end{array}\right]}\ {C}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[o_{i}:\ \begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}Q^{\prime}_{i}\end{array}\right]}{}

Case

o_{i}=\mathit{ok}

We assume some
\theta
,
s^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}
such that
(H3)

\theta,(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\models Q_{i}^{\prime}
. (S4) implies that there is some
(H4a)

(o,\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}_{i},\hat{h}_{i}^{\prime})\in\hat{\Sigma}^{\prime}
such that
(H4b)

\theta,(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})\models\texttt{toAsrt}(\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{\prime}|_{\{\mathsf{ret}\}})
. Define
\varepsilon
as
\varepsilon(x)=\theta(x)
, so by definition of
\hat{\theta}^{\text{id}}_{\varepsilon}
,
(H5)

\varepsilon(\hat{\theta}^{\text{id}}_{\varepsilon})=\theta
.

(S4), (H5) and Thm.
G.3
imply that
(H6)

\varepsilon(\hat{\sigma}^{\prime\prime})=(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})
. Thm.
3
, Thm.
8
, (H4a) and (S3) imply
(H7)

\varepsilon(\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}),\mathsf{C}\Downarrow_{\gamma}\mathit{ok}:(s^{\prime},h^{\prime})
.

Soundness of
produce
, (S1) and (S2) implies
(H8)

\theta,\varepsilon(\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})\models P
.

(S1), (S2) and Prop.
1
of
produce
implies that
\varepsilon(\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})\texttt{.sto}=s
and (S1b) then implies
\theta,\varepsilon(\sigma^{\prime})\models\mathsf{x}=\vec{x}\mathrel{\star}P\mathrel{\star}\vec{\mathsf{z}}=\mathtt{null}
. This concludes the proof of the
\mathit{ok}
case.

Err case.

This case works fully analogously to the

case.

Appendix H
Gillian Evaluation

As explained in §
9
, we ran our freshly implemented UX bi-abduction analysis on the Collections-C library
[
24
]
. In this appendix, we explain what fixes were implemented, provide examples of generated specifications, and detail limitations of the current implementation of Gillian-C, the instantiation of Gillian to the C programming language.

Implemented Fixes.

To obtain our preliminary results, we only implement a subset of all possible fixes for missing errors in the Gillian-C memory model. In particular, we implemented the fixes corresponding to those presented in this paper: we add cells in memory when they are missing during a load, a store, or a
consCell
.

While Gillian-C does feature a variety of other memory operations and consumers, each with their corresponding fixes, our current implementation has proven sufficient in generating a compelling number of interesting specifications.

Examples of Generated Specifications.

We now show examples of specifications generated by our bi-abduction analysis, on a function of the
array
library. In Collections-C, an array structure contains a size and a capacity both of type
size_t
. Then, it contains an expansion factor, which specifies how to increase the capacity of the array when it is full and we need to add a new element. Finally, it contains a pointer to an array of pointers to
void
– an artefact of the lack of polymorphism in C.

The structure definition in the original implementation of the library also contained 3 additional function pointers for users of the library to provide custom implementations of
malloc
,
calloc
and
free
. We removed these pointers from the structure and replaced any calls to these functions to the
stdlib
versions. We performed similar changes to any other structure containing allocator function pointers.

We provide examples for

⬇

typedef

struct

array_s

{

size_t

size
;

size_t

capacity
;

float

exp_factor
;

void

**
buffer
;

//

Modified

to

remove

//

allocator

pointers.

}

Array
;

⬇

cc_enum

array_get_at
(
Array

*
ar
,

size_t

index
,

void

**
out
)

{

if

(
index

>=

ar
->
size
)

return

CC_ERR_OUT_OF_BOUND
;

*
out

=

ar
->
buffer
[
index
];

return

CC_OK
;

}

Gillian is able to generate 9 specifications for this function, 4 of which correspond to successful executions, and 5 to erroneous executions. We detail 2 successful ones and 1 erroneous one, provided in Fig.
14
.

Spec 1: Successful access

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{ar}=a\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{index}=i\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{out}=o\mathrel{\star}}\\
a\texttt{.size}\mapsto s\mathrel{\star}\\
a\texttt{.buffer}\mapsto b\mathrel{\star}(b+i)\mapsto v\mathrel{\star}\\
o\mapsto{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}o^{\prime}}\mathrel{\star}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}o^{\prime}=\mathtt{NULL}}\end{array}\right]}\\
{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}cc\_enum}\ array\_get\_at({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Array}*\!ar,\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}size\_t}\ index,\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}void}*\!*out)}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}a\texttt{.size}\mapsto s\mathrel{\star}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i<s}\mathrel{\star}\\
a\texttt{.buffer}\mapsto b\mathrel{\star}(b+i)\mapsto v\mathrel{\star}\\
o\mapsto{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}v}\mathrel{\star}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}o^{\prime}=\mathtt{NULL}}\mathrel{\star}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{ret}=\mathsf{CC\_OK}}\end{array}\right]}\end{array}

Spec 2: Graceful out-of-bounds

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{ar}=a\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{index}=i\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{out}=o\mathrel{\star}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}o=\mathtt{NULL}}\mathrel{\star}\\
a\texttt{.size}\mapsto s\end{array}\right]}\\
{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}cc\_enum}\ array\_get\_at({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Array}*\!ar,\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}size\_t}\ index,\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}void}*\!*out)}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\mathit{ok}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}o=\mathtt{NULL}}\mathrel{\star}\\
a\texttt{.size}\mapsto s\mathrel{\star}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}i\geq s}\mathrel{\star}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{ret}=\mathsf{CC\_ERR\_OUT\_OF\_BOUNDS}}\end{array}\right]}\end{array}

Spec 3: NULL dereference

\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{ar}=a\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{index}=i\mathrel{\star}\mathsf{out}=o\mathrel{\star}}\\
a=\mathtt{NULL}\mathrel{\star}o=\mathtt{NULL}\end{array}\right]}\\
{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}cc\_enum}\ array\_get\_at({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Array}*\!ar,\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}size\_t}\ index,\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}void}*\!*out)}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\left[\mathit{err}:\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}a=\mathtt{NULL}\mathrel{\star}o=\mathtt{NULL}\mathrel{\star}\\
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{ret}=\mathtt{"segmentation\ fault"}}\end{array}\right]}\end{array}

Figure 14:
Examples of specifications generated by Gillian-C’s bi-abduction

In all three specifications, we highlight in
red
the fragments which are modified between the pre and the post condition, and we
grey out
the mandatory program variable assignments.

Spec 1

corresponds to a successful execution of that function: the size check passes, the accessed cell and the
\mathsf{out}
pointer are properly allocated. The content pointed to by the
\mathsf{out}
pointer is overridden and the function returns
\mathsf{CC\_OK}
. Note the minimal footprint, only the
size
and
buffer
fields of the structure are required and therefore only those fields are bi-abduced.

Spec 2

corresponds to a case where the
size
field of the array structure indicates that the index is out of bounds. The library gracefully handles this by returning an error code.

Spec 3

corresponds to the case where the pointer given as input is a
\mathtt{NULL}
pointer, which triggers a
\mathtt{NULL}
dereference, which Gillian-C detects as an error. This spec is perhaps the most important, as it is the one which, when propagated through the codebase by function calls, would allow a front-end that can filter true bugs to detect an issue.

On Specification Duplication.

Above, we only presented 2 out of the 4 successful specifications generated by Gillian-C. In addition, we highlighted in
purple
extraneous conditions bi-abduced by the Gillian-C engine, in the form
\_=\mathtt{NULL}
. This is due to a current limitation resulting of a bad cocktail mixing the way Gillian-C encodes values in its symbolic heap and information lost in compilation from C to GIL
7
7

7

Gillian-C uses the
CSharpMinor
intermediate language of the CompCert formally verified compiler which has lost most of the typing information available at the source level
: bi-abducing the “shape” of the value (NULL, or not NULL) becomes necessary to preserve well-formedness of the memory.

This phenomenon leads to an explosion in the number of generated specifications, especially when many different memory cells containing pointers are accessed in a row. In particular, Collections-C exposes several iterator structures composed of many pointers, such as
slist_zip_iter
,
hashtable_iter
and
hashet_iter
. For each of these structure, the library also exposes an initialiser function which will assign each field one by one, leading to a path explosion.

Thankfully, this is purely a limitation of the current implementation of Gillian-C, and not of our theoretical framework for bi-abduction, nor of Gillian’s implementation of bi-abduction. It could be overcome if we were to write our own instantiation of Gillian for C, using another compiler and improving the encoding of value in memory. Such work is already in progress, but outside the scope of this paper.

The axiomatic interface for the
consume
and
produce
operations
